TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* Myanmar’s Shan State Is the World’s Meth Lab
  Foreign Policy | 21 Oct 05:00 UTC
  Are illicit drugs now a bigger business in the Mekong than scam centers?
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: The meth business booms in Myanmar’s Shan state while its junta representatives march on Tokyo, Indonesia’s stock market plummets , and Vietnam’s To Lam visits Europe . Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: The meth business booms in Myanmar’s Shan state while its junta representatives march on Tokyo, Indonesia’s stock market plummets , and Vietnam’s To Lam visits Europe . On Oct. 1, police in Laos raided a private jet that was headed for the Philippines with more than half a ton of crystal meth aboard. The smuggling was a polyphonically international affair. The jet was Malaysia-based , owned by a company run by two Malaysian and two Singaporeans. The man who allegedly chartered it is thought to be a politician in south Philippine city of Mindanao. Those arrested in the bust included a Singaporean, a Filipino, a Malaysian, and 10 Lao airport staff. While scam centers now dominate headlines about crime in Southeast Asia, the region’s traditional illicit business—drugs—may well still be bigger. For comparison, in 2023, criminal syndicates operating in the Mekong defrauded victims of some $43.8 billion worldwide, according to a conservative estimate by the U.S. Institute of Peace. Meanwhile, within Southeast Asia alone, sales of meth were worth between $48.1 billion and $74.8 billion annually, according to a report published this year by the United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC). The UNODC found that compared with 2019, when it published its previous report on the topic, drug markets have grown larger—as have the criminal syndicates, which operate in ever more sophisticated ways, protected by state weakness and systemic corruption. For drugs, as with many licit commodities, Southeast Asia is now a major hub for production, transit, and consumption. Drug manufacturing is now concentrated in just one corner of the Golden Triangle: Shan state, in northern Myanmar. Control of the province is split between the Myanmar military and a number of armed rebel groups. The production and sale of drugs has long been a major source of revenue for many of these groups, including the state military. Meth—also known as yaba or shabu, depending on its form—is the key product. The drug is often popular with those working long hours in physically demanding jobs because of its energizing qualities. Meth sales outside the region may also be substantial. R
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/21/myanmar-shan-state-meth-drugs/

* What Does Beijing Mean By ‘Strategic Stability’?
  Foreign Policy | 21 Oct 01:34 UTC
  After the Trump summit, China wants managed competition.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. When U.S. President Donald Trump visited Beijing in May, he and Chinese President Xi Jinping agreed that the U.S. and China should build a “constructive relationship of strategic stability.” At their September meeting in Washington, they reaffirmed that framework and specified that it should rest on respect, fairness, and reciprocity. The leaders were not declaring an end to competition but acknowledging something more realistic: Competition needs a political framework to prevent it from drifting toward confrontation. What exactly is strategic stability supposed to stabilize? When U.S. President Donald Trump visited Beijing in May, he and Chinese President Xi Jinping agreed that the U.S. and China should build a “constructive relationship of strategic stability.” At their September meeting in Washington, they reaffirmed that framework and specified that it should rest on respect, fairness, and reciprocity. The leaders were not declaring an end to competition but acknowledging something more realistic: Competition needs a political framework to prevent it from drifting toward confrontation. What exactly is strategic stability supposed to stabilize? A more useful definition starts with the boundaries of competition: how it is conducted, what ends it serves, and how far it is allowed to spread. The two sides can compete intensely without their chief objective being to undermine each other. They can take security measures without turning every area of economic and technological interaction into a national-security battlefield. And they can disagree sharply over regional issues while keeping channels of communication, and even cooperation, open elsewhere. A focus on stability is not the opposite of competition but is instead the factor that keeps competition from becoming a self-reinforcing escalation. This is why the Washington summit mattered. The Beijing meeting in May established a political framework for the relationship, and the September meeting and its aftermath offer a chance to give that framework greater continuity. The significance of agreeing on a shared goal of stability goes beyond any single agreement. For much of the past decade, China-U.S. diplomacy has too often followed a reactive pattern: Tensions rise, leaders intervene, relations improve, and another dispute interrupts the progress. Xi and Trump’s December 2018 meeting in Buenos Aires produced a trade truce, but tariff escalation resumed in May 2
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/20/china-us-trump-xi-strategic-stability/

The attacks are especially deadly for civilians, with more than 300 killed last month, an 85 percent increase over the year before, and the pace is only intensifying in the first half of October, the United Nations monitoring mission in Ukraine said . The Russian drone-and-missile offensive is meant, like each one before it in every winter of the war, to leave Ukrainians in the dark and cold during winter, in a bid to break popular support for continuing to fight. Moscow launched 76 attacks on energy targets in just the past week. Zelensky said on Monday that the attacks continued over the weekend in about a dozen regions and warned that combined-heat-and-power plants, critical for heating during winter, remain vulnerable. Netanyahu and his religious and far-right allies would capture no more than 49 seats, according to the latest polls . The gaggle of opposition parties united mainly in their desire to remove the prime minister from power would win no more than 54, far from the 61 needed for a parliamentary majority. The rest of the mandates would go mostly to two Arab-led parties, which could theoretically provide the anti-Netanyahu bloc with the margin it needs to govern, except that several members of that bloc have ruled out including these Arab-led parties in a future government. Their availability is critical for services that have become part of people’s daily lives. Millions of users check traffic jams and plan routes, listen to music, watch movies, order groceries, and get answers to questions. Our technologies help hundreds of thousands of companies conduct business: accept orders, serve customers, and interact with partners.” That’s precisely why Ukraine attacked them . Two days later, Kyiv attacked a third one, and after that, it hit the data center belonging to Sberbank, the state-owned Russian bank. And sure enough, despite having had successful confirmation hearings, their nominations had stalled amid partisan fighting. On the night of Sept. 30, the last day that the U.S. Senate was in session before breaking for fall recess, Sen. Jeanne Shaheen strode onto the chamber’s floor and made an unusual request. The 79-year-old Democratic lawmaker sought to force a vote to confirm 10 new ambassadors. The nominees weren’t the big campaign donors who typically get glitzy posts in Paris and Rome. They were career diplomats, tapped to represent Washington in places such as Benin, Sierra Leone, Malaysia, and Kyrgyzstan, whose nominations are frequently held up for political horse-trading. And sure enough, despite having had successful confirmation hearings, their nominations had stalled amid partisan fighting. Fed up with the lack of progress, Shaheen—the top Democrat on the Foreign Relations Committee—made her move. She played the China card. Shaheen gave a speech highlighting the dearth of confirmed U.S. ambassadors while Beijing makes diplomatic inroads around the globe. As she spoke , she gestured to two world maps juxtaposing where the United States and China have ambassadors. While the map of China’s top envoys appeared to be almost filled out, the U.S. map showcased gaping white spaces across most of Africa, with many other unfilled positions in Latin America, Asia, and Europe. Empty posts in Russia and Brazil loomed particularly large. U.S. Sen. Jeanne Shaheen points to a map of filled U.S. ambassadorships as Secretary of State Marco Rubio testifies during a Senate Foreign Relations Committee hearing on Capitol Hill in Washington on Jan. 28. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images Shaheen finished her remarks and requested the confirmation of the nominees. None of the other 99 senators objected—an almost unheard-of result in today’s polarized Senate. “I think this is very uniquely Jeanne Shaheen,” said Naz 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/20/sen-shaheen-democrats-us-midterms-trump-foreign-policy-russia-sanctions/

* UNEP: Opportunity has ‘been lost’ to meet 1.5C warming limit without ‘overshoot’
  Carbon Brief | 21 Oct 05:00 UTC
  A temporary breach of the 1.5C global-warming limit is now “inevitable” following “decades of insufficient climate action”, says the UN… The post UNEP: Opportunity has ‘been lost’ to meet 1.5C warming limit without ‘overshoot’ appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: A temporary breach of the 1.5C global-warming limit is now “inevitable” following “decades of insufficient climate action”, says the UN Environment Programme (UNEP). In its “sobering” annual assessment of the global “emissions gap”, UNEP says that exceeding the Paris Agreement ’s 1.5C limit is “imminent and unavoidable”. This year’s report , titled “The point of return”, says the “task now” is to minimise the size and scale of a temperature “ overshoot ” and to “establish a credible return path to 1.5C by the end of the century”. Even the “lowest possible” overshoot scenario for 1.5C would first see global warming peak at around 1.7-1.8C in the middle of the century before declining, says the report. This scenario – which is “still aligned with the long-term temperature goal of the Paris Agreement” – would require global emissions cuts of 18% by 2030 and 45% by 2035, while ramping up “ removals ” of carbon dioxide (CO2) from the atmosphere, says UNEP. In contrast, it notes that the current climate pledges by countries around the world – even if fully implemented – would only reduce emissions by 5-8% by 2030 and 11-15 % by 2035, relative to 2025 levels. Nonetheless, the report identifies “ample opportunity to accelerate action”, concluding that “countries can still limit peak warming, shorten the duration of overshoot and keep the goals of the Paris Agreement within reach”. (See Carbon Brief’s detailed coverage of previous reports in 2014 , 2015 , 2016 , 2017 , 2018 , 2019 , 2020 , 2021 , 2022 , 2023 , 2024 and 2025 .) The report says that emissions in 2025 were “comparable” to those of 2024. Global greenhouse gas emissions in 2025 totalled 58.4bn tonnes of CO2 equivalent (GtCO2e), according to the report. This is around 0.16GtCO2e lower than 2024 levels – albeit, with high levels of uncertainty around the numbers. In addition, because the figures presented in the 2025 report are “provisional”, the report says, it is “not possible to conclude on the overall direction of emissions in 2025”. The emissions growth rate has been slowing down in recent decades: the growth rate over 1990-2015 was, on average, 1.7% per year, while emissions have increased by an average of 0.8% per year since 2016. The chart below shows the total human-driven emissions between 1990 and 2025, separated by greenhouse gas: CO2 from fossil-fuel combustion (dark grey), methane (light grey), nitrous oxide (blue), fluorinated gases (orange) and CO2 from land-use change (green). Compared to
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/unep-opportunity-has-been-lost-to-meet-1-5c-warming-limit-without-overshoot

* Q&A: Why a pivotal IPCC meeting in Ethiopia ended in ‘profound disappointment’
  Carbon Brief | 20 Oct 14:40 UTC
  Countries have failed – for the sixth time running – to agree on the timeline for publishing the Intergovernmental Panel… The post Q&A: Why a pivotal IPCC meeting in Ethiopia ended in ‘profound disappointment’ appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Countries have failed – for the sixth time running – to agree on the timeline for publishing the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ’s (IPCC) next set of influential reports. The IPCC’s 65th session (IPCC-65) was seen by many to be the final opportunity for countries to agree on a schedule where its seventh assessment report (AR7) would be published in time for the UN’s second “ global stocktake ” of climate action. Before last week’s meeting in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, IPCC chair Prof Sir Jim Skea had warned that releasing the reports in time for the stocktake in 2028 would depend on a timeline being agreed by the end of 2026. But the week-long meeting descended into fraught disagreements between governments, resulting in Skea “suspending” the talks on Friday evening with no decision made. Countries were also unable to agree on the IPCC’s budget for the rest of the AR7 cycle, leaving the body with a “serious funding crisis”. The IPCC has pledged to reconvene the meeting at the “earliest opportunity”; however, it has not yet given an indication of when this will be. In his concluding remarks, Skea described the Addis Ababa summit as a “profound disappointment” and “among the most difficult IPCC meetings”, according to reporting from the Earth Negotiations Bulletin (ENB). Some countries have reacted with dismay to the breakdown of negotiations, warning that a failure to have the IPCC’s latest assessments on climate adaptation and mitigation ready for the UN stocktaking exercise would undermine global climate action. A climate envoy from a Pacific island state said it was a “travesty that a handful of countries continue to delay the IPCC’s reports – the science we need back home to help us save lives and livelihoods”. Below, Carbon Brief explores the key developments, debates and disagreements at the IPCC meeting. Top of the agenda during the Addis Ababa meeting was a decision on the timeline of the publication of AR7’s three working group reports – also known as the “work plan”. The three reports cover climate science (WG1), impacts and adaptation (WG2) and mitigation (WG3). Countries failed to reach a resolution on the scheduling of the influential reports at five consecutive IPCC meetings since 2024 – despite drafting being well underway. (For more, see Carbon Brief’s write-up of discussions in Istanbul , Sofia , Hangzhou , Lima and Bangkok .) * Microsoft, Alphabet, Goldman Sachs Join Growing List of Companies Objecting to Tokenized Stocks
  Decrypt | 20 Oct 20:16 UTC
  Sixteen companies have now objected to having their shares traded on the planned OKX-ICE tokenized stock venue, up from nine just four days earlier, according to an updated public notice.
  FULL TEXT: The corporate backlash against tokenized stocks is snowballing. Seven more companies, including Microsoft, Google parent Alphabet, and Goldman Sachs, have objected to having their shares traded on OKXICE TSV, according to an updated public notice dated Monday. OKXICE TSV is a planned tokenized stock venue from OKX and NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange. Qualcomm, Caterpillar, McDonald’s, and Western Digital also joined the list. That brings the total to 16 objecting issuers, up from nine in the venue’s previous notice just four days earlier. The earlier group included Verizon, Broadcom, UnitedHealth Group, CoreWeave and Rocket Lab. Neither notice explains the companies’ reasons or whether objecting keeps a stock off the platform. Goldman’s inclusion stands out, given the bank’s own investments in blockchain-based markets. The updated notice also contains a quiet but notable change. The Oct. 15 version said the venue would offer stocks tokenized by third parties unaffiliated with the companies. Monday’s version says it will offer stocks tokenized by either an outside party or the issuer itself, potentially giving companies a more direct role in how their shares appear onchain. Under the third-party model, an outside “tokenizer” buys and holds real shares one-for-one through a registered broker-dealer, then issues tokens meant to carry the same dividend and voting rights, according to the notice. The venue plans to run 24/7 on Uniswap v4 liquidity pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, with trades settled in stablecoins such as USDC and USDT. OKXICE is set to operate under the SEC’s innovation exemption , which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate. The exemption lets venues trade tokenized U.S. stocks without registering as exchanges, and the notice acknowledges OKXICE isn’t subject to Regulation NMS or fair-access rules. OKX and ICE first joined forces on tokenized securities earlier this year before unveiling the 24/7 venue this month. Nasdaq, which won SEC approval for its own tokenized securities pilot, has said it will opt its shares out of the exemption. A Nasdaq spokesperson previously told The Wall Street Journal that it doesn’t want its stock trading on a venue that doesn’t show the best prices in real time or report trades to the consolidated tape. The IMF recently warned that tokenized stock markets remain volatile and illiquid despite real demand. Start every day with the top news stories right now, plus origi
  https://decrypt.co/381448/microsoft-alphabet-goldman-sachs-growing-list-objecting-tokenized-stocks

* Morning Minute: Nine Blue Chip Companies Say No to Tokenized Stocks
  Decrypt | 20 Oct 12:06 UTC
  If companies are objecting, does that deflate the tokenized stock hopes before they begin to materialize? And who is impacted the most?
  FULL TEXT: Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner . The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And c heck out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action. Tokenized stocks have been one of the hottest topics in crypto this year. But now, the companies behind the shares are starting to object. Nine issuers have objected to their shares trading on OKXICE , the tokenized stock venue from OKX and ICE, the company that owns the New York Stock Exchange. The companies include Verizon, Broadcom, UnitedHealth, Cerebras, AppLovin, Applied Digital, Rocket Lab, CoreWeave and Applied Optoelectronics. That’s 14% of the 63 companies the venue planned to list, and unfortunately, the notice doesn’t say why they objected or whether it keeps them off. The OKX venue itself is ambitious, featuring around-the-clock trading through Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer chain, settled in USDC and USDT, with a third-party tokenizer holding the real shares one-for-one at a registered broker-dealer. It exists because the SEC unveiled an “Innovation Exemption” in September, after the Clarity Act died in the Senate, letting platforms trade tokenized stocks without registering as exchanges. But here’s the rub. OKXICE isn’t registered with the SEC and isn’t subject to Regulation NMS, the rule ensuring investors get the best displayed price. It isn’t required to give fair access. Trading is capped at a fraction of each stock’s normal volume. And administrators can upgrade the smart contracts with no advance notice. Nasdaq isn’t on the list but is opting its own shares out anyway, citing the lack of real-time best prices and consolidated tape reporting. Nasdaq just won approval for its own tokenized securities pilot, so the company building one venue won’t put its stock on a rival’s. Here’s the problem nobody priced in: issuer consent. Every bull case assumed the hard part was regulatory. Get permission, build the rails, the assets arrive. But a tokenized share needs a real share behind it, and now the company gets a say. A CFO weighing a thinner, more volatile market trading their stock at 3am with no best-execution duty can choose to opt out. And some already are. So who is impacted? OKXICE most directly, losing 14% of its roster before it has even announced a launch date. Robinhood, Kraken and Backed Finance are hit too, all running stock-token products that need a growing pool of tokeniza
  https://decrypt.co/381399/morning-minute-nine-blue-chip-companies-say-no-to-tokenized-stocks

The “student,” who sported an Algerian and Senegalese flag alongside his name on Snapchat, wanted to break some things — everything, in fact. We’re gonna break everything. Add me to the blockade group, thank you,” followed by two Algerian flag emojis. The short message was so full of absurdities, anachronisms and racist assumptions that the recipient knew at once it wasn’t from a fellow student. It was, they suspected, from the police, trying to infiltrate their organizing network and conjuring their own ideas of how kids talked and behaved to do it. Later reporting from La Provence indicated they weren’t too far off: The geolocation of the original message indicated that it likely came from a police station in Aix-en-Provence, the news site reported. Beyond the meme, however, is the reality of the way the French state views its young citizens , particularly those of Arab, Maghrebi and African descent. The issue that drove students to the streets — underfunded schools where buildings are falling apart and teacher absenteeism is rampant — impacts Black and Arab neighborhoods at higher rates.   New Lines | 20 Oct 11:06 UTC
  When an anonymous X account alleged that the it-book of the year in France was likely AI-generated, the scandal threw the literary world into an existential crisis. But under the veneer of integrity lay deep connections to the far right. The post The French Far Right Has Opened Pangram’s Box appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: The question was posed at 4:39 p.m. on Monday, Sept. 21, by @Pangramed, an anonymous X account with just 92 followers. It was accompanied by a picture of Thélyson Orélien’s hit debut novel, “It Was Either That or Die” (“C’Était Ça ou Mourir”). Since its French release in August, the novel and its 38-year-old Haitian-born Canadian author have taken the literary world by storm. The story of a schoolteacher who flees gang rule in Haiti and crosses 12 countries to reach Canada, the novel was the book of the “rentrée littéraire,” France’s avalanche of new fiction for the autumn literary season. It had already won two prizes, was shortlisted for the Goncourt — France’s most prestigious literary prize, awarded to the likes of Marcel Proust and Simone de Beauvoir — and had seduced legions of readers. Among them was the mayor of Beziers, Robert Ménard, who has often been accused of turning his city into a laboratory for the far right. He said it brought him to “rethink his view of migrants.” But the X post wasn’t another glowing review from a newly converted reader. It was the undoing of a career on a meteoric rise, in a matter of 1,200 characters. The novel was, the post claimed, “written almost entirely by artificial intelligence.” @Pangramed brought what they claimed were the receipts: screenshots from Pangram, an AI-detection program the account described as “the most reliable” on the market, suggesting that “almost 100%” of the book had been written by AI. Newsrooms across the political spectrum fed “It Was Either That or Die” into other AI detection sites, with conflicting results. For Pangram’s false positive rates, it should be noted, are not negligible. The company estimates that its overall false positive rate is 1 in 10,000, though other factors, including the language of the text, can affect that rate. Within days, the Goncourt Academy nonetheless struck the novel from its shortlist. The Orélien scandal, as it would come to be known, quickly took France by storm. Mainstream coverage focused obsessively on existential questions around the future of literature, with AI-detection tools held up as the now unavoidable sentinels of the (human-)written word. As a safety measure, the other 15 books shortlisted for the Goncourt were fed into Pangram as well. * How intuitions work
  Aeon | 20 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Just as seeing a tree gives you reason to believe it’s there, sensing that a stranger is creepy gives you reason to walk away - by Marc A Moffett Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: At the Campidoglio, Rome, 1984. Photo by Richard Kalvar/Magnum When people say such things, they are not typically drawing conclusions from a chain of reasoning. The person who avoids getting in the stranger’s car is not thinking: ‘He displayed these three behavioural cues and, therefore, he poses a risk.’ The email isn’t flagged by going through an explicit checklist. The deal doesn’t announce its flaws through a formal audit. In each case, something is simply presented as having a certain character: the man as creepy, the email as fraudulent, the deal as suspect. And yet, acting on these intuitions seems not merely excusable but entirely reasonable . If the man really does strike you as creepy, staying out of his car was probably the smart call – even if you can’t say why. Despite this, ‘vibes’ and ‘intuitions’ are often dismissed both in daily life and in academic enquiry as irrational or merely heuristic devices. And while we cannot always trust such reactions, they are central, indeed indispensable, to our ability to rationally navigate the world. Philosophers, however, have done surprisingly little to illuminate the rational credentials of these ordinary kinds of intuitions. Instead, they have focused the bulk of their attention on a much more rarefied kind of intuition called rational intuition , which is taken to function primarily in the a priori disciplines of philosophy, logic and mathematics. My objective is to explain why our everyday intuitive reactions can have such rational force. The key is to see that intuition works in much the same way as perception, in that both can directly present something to us as actual. Once we understand what gives perception this feature, we can see why intuitions might involve exercising the same basic kind of cognitive capacity. A standard way of characterising rational intuition is in terms of seemings , by analogy with perception. Just as it perceptually seems to you that there is a car in the garage, so it can intellectually seem to you that 2 + 2 = 4 – the former presenting a physical fact, the latter a conceptual one. But rational intuition and the everyday intuition at work when you decline to get in a stranger’s car are not as far apart as this rarefied example suggests: the philosopher’s intuition is, at bottom, a highly specialised version of the same basic capacity. To draw these two capacities together, let’s consider a favourite pastime of philosophers: the thought experiment. Philosophers have a 
  https://aeon.co/essays/sometimes-you-just-know-somethings-off-but-how

  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Fatih Mubarok/AFP via Getty Images) The Egyptian Parliament will vote next Monday on whether to authorize President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi to send military support to Saudi Arabia, following two days of debate in the legislature that began on the weekend. Sisi asked parliament to hold an emergency session to authorize him “to take any measure [needed] to protect Saudi Arabia,” he said on Saturday during a speech at the graduation ceremony at the Military Academy. “I am speaking here not to attack anyone, but only to defend” Saudi Arabia, he said, referring to the escalating attacks the kingdom is facing at the hands of the Houthi militia. According to the Egyptian Constitution, the president cannot declare war or send armed forces into combat outside the state’s territory without consulting the National Defense Council and gaining the support of a two-thirds majority in the House of Representatives. Egypt’s National Defense Council, headed by Sisi, approved providing Saudi Arabia with “all necessary and possible forms of support” on Sunday, without specifying their nature. On the same day, the parliament held a closed session to discuss Sisi’s request, but extended the sessions “due to the large number of speakers requesting the floor,” Mostafa Bakry, a parliamentarian, said in a post on X. Bakry stressed that Egypt is still keen to seek a political solution. Officials agreed to continue talks on an initiative that aims to guarantee a halt to “hostile actions” against Saudi Arabia and other Arab countries, ensuring safety and freedom of maritime routes and initiating a process to reach a political solution to the crisis. Bakry added that, next Monday, the parliament will take a position that reaffirms Egypt’s “firm and principled” solidarity with Saudi Arabia. On the other side of that same river—a river with two names, Río Bravo on one bank, Rio Grande on the other—men were falling from gunfire while children went without food so that revolutionaries could have the sustenance they needed to keep fighting. * Yemen’s Salafist Commanders Are Shaping Its Battlefield
  New Lines | 19 Oct 10:52 UTC
  Three Salafist commanders who rose from Yemen’s preaching circles are leading the Saudi-backed push to retake the west coast, and for Gen. Abdulrahman al-Mahrami, the campaign is a test of whether his battlefield reputation can translate to the national stage. The post Yemen’s Salafist Commanders Are Shaping Its Battlefield appeared first...
  FULL TEXT: Gen. Abdulrahman al-Mahrami, known as Abu Zaraa, is one of the most visible figures in Yemen’s military and media landscape today. Alongside others, he is leading Operation Dawn of Yemen, a campaign aimed at retaking the west coast region on the Red Sea, which the Houthis seized in September, in their biggest military and geopolitical victory in years. Al-Mahrami’s involvement is not simply a matter of his role as commander of the Southern Giants Brigades, or his seat on the Presidential Leadership Council (PLC), Yemen’s internationally recognized government. It is also the region where he made his name nine years ago in a previous fight to drive the Houthis out. Born in 1980 in the Rasad-Yafaa district in southern Yemen, al-Mahrami is fighting to protect his most significant military achievement, one that underpinned his subsequent rise as both a military and political figure. Two other commanders — Brig. Gen. Hamdi Shukri al-Subaihi and Maj. Gen. Abdulrahman al-Lahji — share both his record on the west coast and his religious background as a Salafist, the conservative brand of Sunni Islam that models itself on the behavior of the Prophet Muhammad’s earliest companions. In the first operation to wrest the region from the Houthis, between January 2017 and November 2018, the United Arab Emirates oversaw the campaign. Saudi Arabia is overseeing the current one, with all three men once again involved. Together, they have come to encapsulate the battle for Yemen as it currently stands, with its shifting battlefronts and the carnage it is now wreaking in both Yemen itself and Saudi Arabia. * Horrible, unmanly, indispensable
  Aeon | 19 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Renaissance Italians saw the gun as the Devil’s work. But soon they embraced this unchivalrous – and necessary – weapon - by Catherine Fletcher Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Detail from The Battle of Pavia, 1525 ( c 1529) by Robert Heller. Courtesy the Nationalmuseum, Sweden /Wikipedia If you were asked to think of a gun owner, then Lorenzo de’ Medici – the celebrated patron of Renaissance art – would probably not be the first person to come to mind. In fact, however, Lorenzo ‘the Magnificent’ owned five portable firearms, which are listed in his 1492 postmortem inventory. Guns and gunpowder were controversial. In art and literature, they were labelled the Devil’s work, and were perceived as fundamentally unchivalrous. Yet within half a century, by the early months of 1539, Lorenzo’s descendant Duke Cosimo de’ Medici had 91 firearms in his Florentine storerooms. Somehow, in the courts of late-Renaissance Italy, the gun had become an acceptable, even valued, weapon. Beyond the palazzi of the peninsula’s towns and cities, guns were also making inroads into more ordinary lives. There were no standing armies in the 16th-century Italian states. Their wars were fought through temporary mobilisations, and with considerable help from companies of mercenaries. Yet as the 1506 provision that established the Florentine militia made clear, ‘little hope’ could be placed ‘in foreign and hired arms’. Far better to establish a new magistracy with ‘full authority and power’ to enlist men from Florentine territory, at least 10,000 of them. One in 10 of these men were to be equipped with firearms, and 500 handguns ( schioppetti ) should be kept in the Palazzo della Signoria (the city hall, now known as the Palazzo Vecchio) along with ‘such money as should be necessary for the schioppetti ’, presumably to purchase gunpowder and shot. Three or four companies ( bandiere ) entirely of schioppettieri might also be established. This militia, most often noted for the role of Niccolò Machiavelli in its foundation, was to be organised after the fashion of the Germans, in whose cities there was a centuries-old tradition of civic defence and a duty on every male citizen to maintain suitable arms for the purpose. In fact, one of the Florentine militia’s early constables was German (a Giovanni Tedesco); another (Giliberto Spagnolo) came from Spain, a country making the running in the use of small arms on the battlefield. Giovanni Tedesco arranged target practice for his company, which from 1508 was extended more generally among the militia, with exercises taking place on a monthly basis. M ilitias were not new but, by the 1490s, large-scale foreign military

Now in her mid-80s, Hala had not been to the palace since her father’s last term (1955-1958), and would probably never have returned if not for Assad’s ouster in December 2024. From his deathbed in 1967, Quwatli bequeathed his memoirs to his two sons and three daughters. The documents were stored in Beirut, only to be partially damaged during the 1982 Israeli invasion of Lebanon and then moved to a bank in France, where they remained until mid-2025. * How to be a (Roman) man
  Aeon | 16 Oct 10:00 UTC [also carried by: Longreads]
  Cicero’s ideal of manly virtue stressed the performance of self-control and bravery, not boastful egotism and domination - by Catharine Edwards Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Roman bust, mid-1st century CE. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York What does it mean to be a man? In the early 21st century, masculinity is contested and fraught with anxiety; influencers offer models of manliness involving fast cars, sexual conquests, and building up muscle. Before the rise of feminism, they suggest, in some supposed Golden Age, everyone had a shared sense of what it meant to be a man – and being a man was unproblematically good. The Roman Empire, in their view, offers men today an inspirational example of power, hierarchy and achievement, which is why men think about it so much – unlike women, who are too immersed in their feelings… Manliness certainly was celebrated in the works of Cicero, republican Rome’s most eloquent statesman. In the mid-1st century BCE, his persuasive powers in the law courts and the senate were second to none; his speeches went on to inspire would-be orators well into the 20th century. He is often seen as the very embodiment of the Roman republic. The political world in which he flourished was a self-consciously masculine one; to be accused of acting like a woman was, for a Roman man, a grievous insult. Yet in Cicero’s writing we can find other strains too. For Roman men, anything labelled feminine was necessarily to be avoided, something to be ashamed of. But what exactly did it mean to act like a man? Ancient Rome in the 2nd and 1st centuries BCE saw the rise of a succession of military leaders who were promoted as masculine role models. The great general Marius won a series of victories in Spain, North Africa and on Italy’s northern frontier. For several years in a row, he was elected consul, one of Rome’s two chief executive officers. Unlike many other Roman politicians, he didn’t have an aristocratic background. One Roman writer imagines Marius fending off accusations that he was coarse in his manners: Here was a model of manliness forged on the battlefield. Given the military might of ancient Rome, it is surely unsurprising that Romans often associated masculinity with courage in battle. The word virtus , a term cognate with the Roman vir (meaning man as opposed to woman), clearly means ‘courage in war’ when it is first recorded in the mid-5th century BCE. Courage in battle could be taken as the foremost manifestation of Roman masculinity.   https://aeon.co/essays/the-politics-of-masculinity-at-the-end-of-the-roman-republic

  FULL TEXT: Taiz governorate in southwestern Yemen was never simply another front in the country’s war. The seven-day lightning offensive in September — in which the Houthis seized the strategic port city of Mokha and the Bab al-Mandab Strait between the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden — amounted to more than a change on a map. And several districts in the south, west and east of Taiz governorate collapsed between Sept. 13 and Oct. 10, extending Houthi control to additional strategic areas and consolidating their gains on Yemen’s western coast. Taiz governorate has been one of the most important centers of resistance to the Houthis throughout the conflict. Its demographic, political and historical weight means that battles there have an importance that extends far beyond the governorate itself. If the Houthis consolidate their hold on what remains of the countryside and the city of Taiz itself, they could reshape the balance of power in Yemen. Recent events reveal a painful paradox: Taiz was a popular base of support for the republican system, which was born in 1962 following the overthrow of the theocratic monarchy that ruled the country. The population also had combat experience. But locals failed to forge a unified military institution capable of protecting the area and honoring its sacrifices. The recent collapse was not simply the result of a shortage of fighters or a lack of will to fight; it was also the product of failures in command, coordination and supply, and the subordination of nationwide institution-building to partisan and personal interests. For roughly 12 years, Taiz governorate remained a major center of resistance, even while the Houthis controlled parts of the city of Taiz and large stretches of its countryside, including al-Hawban, the governorate’s most important economic and commercial center. But the endurance of its people and fighters never translated into a sustainable defense system. The front lines remained exposed to fragmented decision-making, competing interests and weak coordination, until the nightmare of a renewed siege returned. The question today is not only whether the Houthis will be able to take the city of Taiz, but what this development would mean for Yemen’s future. Would it represent a military setback that can be contained, or a strategic shift that could redraw the balance of power in western Yemen, weaken the anti-Houthi camp and deepen the divide between north and south? A machine may do some of that work faster, but each act it takes over is one that the body no longer performs and that the body, including the brain, no longer learns through.   FULL TEXT: By studying fire scars in ancient wood, many scientists have become convinced that Indigenous people transformed North America long before European colonizers arrived. M.R. O’Connor spends time with dendrochronologists, who study tree rings, and other experts to understand what they’re learning: Anthropologist Omer Stewart, who began documenting Native American burning practices in the nineteen-thirties . . . found that Indigenous communities burned prairies to attract bison to fresh grass and to maintain oak “orchards” where food could be gathered. He became convinced that Native Americans had reshaped the geography, and in some cases the climate, of North American ecosystems. Their impact “was profound, in terms of the amount of land converted to agriculture, the species they hunted and fished, and most particularluthe way they shaped their world through the use of fire,” the naturalist Scott Weidensaul wrote, in his 2012 book The First Frontier . Weidensaul argued that early America “was fruitful beyond modern recognition, it was riotous in its natural wealth, but it was not wilderness.” The dendrochronologist Evan Larson, at the University of Wisconsin-Platteville, made a similar claim: settlers encountered rich landscapes because Native Americans were tending them. “Whenever you’re talking about fire, you’re engaging with a central narrative around this untamed frontier and wilderness,” Larson told me. “In fact, that’s never been true. The early success of pioneers was already benefitting from the labor of somebody else.” “Nature writing after everyone stopped paying attention.” “Godwits make the longest non-stop migration in the bird world. * Beyond the Glass
  Longreads | 15 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Among a collection’s many skulls, a grieving son learns how to live in a world that keeps going.
  FULL TEXT: Daniel Barnum | Longreads | October 15, 2026 | 4,116 words (15 minutes) This essay is co-published with the literary magazine AGNI and appears in this fall’s AGNI 104 . The Hyrtl Skull Collection is the first exhibit you notice when you walk into the Mütter Museum at the College of Physicians of Philadelphia. This is by curatorial design: The skulls face the main entrance, staring steadily at each person who crosses the threshold. All 139 are evenly spaced in long lines behind the glass doors of a towering cherrywood cabinet. Each one rests in a wire frame on top of a thin balsa box with a tag at its center. I recall tracking them with glances as I shuffled nearby with two friends, as part of a large crowd. Small bright lights illuminated them from inside the cases, making them glow dully from my angle. Seeing them for the first time, I was both mesmerized by this effect and intimidated by the skulls’ great number. I tried not to look for too long, but the density of people on the narrow walkway made that impossible: I was soon stuck in the dead center of the exhibit. Almost in spite of myself, I scanned the wall text and learned how they had been brought together to disprove a piece of 19th-century race science, which seemed like one of the better reasons to collect skulls. Most of the exhibits in the Mütter Museum have similarly odd origins. Tucked into a corner of the College of Physicians’ stately New Beaux-Arts building, the Mütter is a 19th-century medical teaching collection/cabinet museum with an annual visitorship of almost 150,000 people. Its staff goes on late-night talk shows with specimens in tow, hosts YouTube series. The gift shop sells soaps and keychains in the shapes of its artifacts. Coming out of a death-obsessed past into an incongruous living present, the exhibits are caught somewhere between scientific exemplar and exploitative spectacle. The museum acknowledges its reputation as a freak show and, in response, doubles down on the meanings of curiosity. Signs we saw at the main gate declared the place’s ethos: disturbingly informative . The text beside the skulls explained itself: Brought together is a euphemistic way of saying, assembled by grave robbing . Dr. Hyrtl dug up a lot of his collection, or had assistants do it for him. Or paid other collectors, other doctors. The graves were attached to hospitals, madhouses, prisons. Some of the people these skulls belonged to surely had surviving relatives, though none could afford or car
  https://longreads.com/2026/10/15/mother-museum-skulls-grief-agni/

* What the West can learn from AI optimists in the rest of the world
  Rest of World | 15 Oct 10:00 UTC
  The developing world’s enthusiastic adoption of AI and necessity-driven innovations offer lessons for an anxiety-ridden West.
  FULL TEXT: On a recent Sunday afternoon, I was invited to debate AI’s impact on the cultural sector alongside Dutch politicians and creative-industry leaders. I was the lone optimist, arguing that if fear and risk become our primary lens for governing artificial intelligence, we will design systems around what we want to prevent rather than what we want to use them for. Then I said something that was harder for the room to hear: The Global South is home to some of AI’s most enthusiastic and inventive users, and their experiments could help us rethink our own institutions. In the West, fear of AI has helped turn pessimism into a cultural virtue. The U.S. is no different from Europe. A culture of fear encourages protectionism: We hunker down and romanticize legacy systems rather than ask what might replace them. Creative industries in the majority world have spent decades navigating piracy and weak enforcement, forcing them to think beyond rights-based policing about how creators actually receive value. Anghami , the biggest music app in the Middle East, for instance, pays musicians from their first stream, rather than only after a song reaches a minimum threshold. It has approached AI pragmatically, using it to curate, tag, and attribute creative work rather than simply blocking and banning. In the West, fear of AI has helped turn pessimism into a cultural virtue. Would you publicly say you love AI? Your answer may say something about where you’re from. Say “yes” to a concerned audience in the U.S. or the Netherlands, and you risk being seen as naive, or complicit in extraction and exploitation. While the U.S. is building and deploying AI at a scale no other country matches, Americans are deeply anxious about where it is heading. Only 17% of U.S. adults think AI will have a positive impact on the country over the next 20 years, according to a 2025 Pew Research Center survey . In much of the Global South, the mood is markedly different . Stanford’s 2025 AI Index found that 83% of respondents in China, 80% in Indonesia, and 77% in Thailand said AI-powered products offer more benefits than drawbacks, compared with 39% in the U.S. and 36% in the Netherlands. Demographics are a part of the answer. Nearly 90% of the world’s young people live outside the West. They are not simply using AI, they are playing with it. * The ladder of knowledge
  Aeon | 15 Oct 10:00 UTC
  What we claim as ‘true’ is not a ceiling we are forever failing to reach but something that changes as we climb toward it - by Alexander V Gheorghiu Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Hamburg, Germany, 1937. Photo by Herbert List/Magnum Photos On the evening of 8 September 1930, the German mathematician David Hilbert stepped up to a microphone in Königsberg and addressed his countrymen by radio. He was 68, recently retired, and at the height of his fame. The talk was a defence of pure reason, delivered in the city of Immanuel Kant, and it ended with two phrases that would later be carved on his tombstone: Wir müssen wissen. Wir werden wissen . We must know. We shall know. David Hilbert. Courtesy the University of Göttingen Are certain questions closed to the human mind? Are there truths about the world that we can never know? Hilbert thought that, at least within mathematics, this could not be the case. For him, mathematics was a game played with symbols: one could fix the right axioms as rules of the game and every mathematical problem could be solved. He was wrong. The day before his Königsberg address, at a small academic meeting in the same city, a 24-year-old Austrian named Kurt Gödel had quietly announced that no such system for arithmetic could exist. Whatever reasonable set of rules you fix, something true must lie beyond them and thus beyond our knowledge. We can’t know. We won’t know. Gödel’s results, known as the Incompleteness Theorems, are landmark achievements of 20th-century logic. Recent work in philosophy and mathematics has begun to illuminate the gap they opened, and with it something deeper about the foundations of knowledge itself. The story begins in the same city, but two centuries earlier. W hen Kant remembered a book by David Hume he’d read years earlier, he was woken from what he called his ‘dogmatic slumber’. Kant had believed that pure reasoning could teach us every absolute truth about the world, but Hume had asked a question that that stumped him: what reason do we actually have to believe that the future will resemble the past? We may have seen the Sun rise a thousand times, but how do we know it will rise tomorrow? The belief that the future will resemble the past is, Hume argued, merely a kind of habit. Kant couldn’t answer Hume’s challenge and was silenced for a decade. For Hume, proving a theorem was like unpacking a suitcase to find an outfit. Kant could not leave it there When Kant finally broke his silence with the Critique of Pure Reason (1781), he offered a radical answer. We can know the world only as it appears to our minds, and that is where our science sits; the noumenal world – the Ding an si
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-century-long-search-for-the-limits-to-knowledge

The adolescent quest for self-discovery is timeless—but today’s parents experimented in ways that were analog: new hairstyles, inscrutable music, beer snuck in the woods. Their own parents might not have liked it, but they could at least relate. Now the landscape of adolescent discovery is literally foreign, thanks to social media, which has turbocharged once-obscure subcultures like the furry fandom—and, at its rarer edge, therianthropy—and redefined what “fringe” even means. Even if a kid is the only therian at school, she can find 400 more people just like her online. That’s one of the biggest challenges for parents in the algorithm age: The world your kid actually lives in is one you can’t enter.
